Side Conditions
:

{\dagger}_{1}:=

w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u

{\dagger}_{2}:=

w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
u\normalsize\not\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v

{\dagger}_{3}:=

w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
w\normalsize\not\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v

Figure 3:
Admissible rules.

In this section, we show that
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
and
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
satisfy a sizable number of favorable properties culminating in syntactic cut-elimination. We explain here some key steps; the full details are available in Appendix
B
.

\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
and
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
can be seen as first-order extensions of Postniece’s deep-nested sequent calculus for bi-intuitionistic logic
\mathrm{DBiInt}

[
30
,
13
]
.
Cut-elimination for
\mathrm{DBiInt}

[
13
]
was proven in two stages. First, cut-elimination was proven for a “shallow” version of the nested sequent calculus
\mathrm{LBiInt}
, which can be seen as a variant of a display calculus
[
1
]
. The cut-elimination proof for this shallow calculus follows from Belnap’s generic cut-elimination for display calculi
[
1
]
. Second, cut-free proofs in the shallow calculus are shown to be translatable to proofs in the deep-nested calculus. We do not have the corresponding shallow versions of
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
and
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
, so we cannot rely on Belnap’s generic cut-elimination. It may be possible to define shallow versions of our calculi, and then follow the same methodology outlined in
[
13
]
to prove cut-elimination, but we find that a direct cut-elimination proof is simpler, e.g., it avoids the need for proving the admissibility of certain structural rules called the display postulates
[
1
]
, which lets one transition from shallow to deep inference systems.

Since our polytree sequents are a restriction of ordinary labeled sequents, another possible approach to cut-elimination is to apply the methodology for labeled sequent calculi
[
27
]
. A main issue in adapting this methodology is ensuring that the proof transformations needed in cut-elimination preserve the polytree structure of sequents. A key proof transformation in a typical cut-elimination proof for labeled calculi is label substitution: given a proof
\pi_{1}
and labels
u
and
w
, one constructs another proof
\pi_{2}
by replacing
u
with
w
everywhere in
\pi_{1}
and adjusts the inference rules accordingly. This is typically needed in the reduction of a cut where the last rules in both branches of the cut apply to the cut formula, and where one of the rules introduces (reading the rule bottom up) a new label and a new relational atom (e.g.,
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
). Such a substitution operation may not preserve polytree structures. Another notable difference between our calculi and traditional labeled calculi is the absence of structural rules manipulating relational atoms. These differences mean that cut-elimination techniques for labeled sequent calculi cannot be immediately applied in our setting.

Instead, our cut-elimination proof builds on an approach by Pinto and Uustalu
[
28
,
29
]
, which deals with a polytree sequent calculus for
propositional
bi-intuitionistic logic. We thus provide a series of proof transformations, culminating in the elimination of cuts, which shares similarities with their work in the propositional case and expands in the first-order direction. These transformations are captured in proofs of the admissibility of rules shown in Figure
3
. We illustrate some key transformations and why they are needed, through an example of a cut where
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
and
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
are applied to the cut formula. The formal details are available in the proof of Theorem
30
.

Suppose we have the instance of cut shown below left, where
\pi_{1}
is shown below right and
\pi_{2}
is shown below bottom with
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u.

\pi_{1}

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Delta

\pi_{2}

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi\vdash\Delta

cut

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta

\pi_{1}^{\prime}

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},wRw^{\prime},\Gamma,w^{\prime}:\varphi\vdash w^{\prime}:\psi,\Delta

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Delta

\pi_{3}

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u:\varphi

\pi_{4}

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,u:\psi\vdash\Delta

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi\vdash\Delta

A typical cut reduction strategy would be to cut
\pi_{1}
with
\pi_{3}
and
\pi_{4}
(both with cut formula
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
), producing the proofs
\pi_{5}
and
\pi_{6}
of
\mathcal{R},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u:\varphi
and
\mathcal{R},\Gamma,u:\psi\vdash\Delta
, respectively. Next, one would cut
\pi_{5}
with
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
(with cut formula
\varphi
), producing a proof
\pi_{7}
, and then cut
\pi_{7}
with
\pi_{6}
(with cut formula
\psi
). There are a couple of issues with this strategy: (1) the cut formulae in the last two instances of cut have mismatched labels, i.e.,
w^{\prime}
on one side and
u
on the other ; (2) the label
w^{\prime}
and the relational atom
wRw^{\prime}
are not present in the conclusions of
\pi_{5}
and
\pi_{6}
, so the contexts of the premises of the cuts do not match.

To fix these issues, we first need to transform the proof
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
into two proofs
\pi_{5}
and
\pi_{6}
, shown below left and right, respectively. As shown in the cut-elimination proof, a transformation that we use in this case is one that is represented by the rule
(iw)
. This ensures that the contexts match the contexts of the concluding sequents in
\pi_{3}
and
\pi_{4}
.

\pi_{1}^{\prime}

\mathcal{R},wRw^{\prime},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}:\varphi\vdash w^{\prime}:\psi,\Delta

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Delta

(iw)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,u:\varphi,\Delta

\pi_{1}^{\prime}

\mathcal{R},wRw^{\prime},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}:\varphi\vdash w^{\prime}:\psi,\Delta

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Delta

(iw)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:\varphi\rightarrow\psi,u:\psi\vdash\Delta

We then cut
\pi_{3}
and
\pi_{4}
with
\pi_{5}
and
\pi_{6}
, respectively, which yields proofs
\pi_{7}
and
\pi_{8}
of
\mathcal{R},\Gamma\vdash u:\varphi,\Delta
and
\mathcal{R},\Gamma,u:\psi\vdash\Delta
, respectively. At this stage, we want to cut
\pi_{7}
with
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
, and then cut the resulting proof with
\pi_{8}
. However, this cut cannot be performed until the label
w^{\prime}
and its associated relational atom are removed from the conclusion of
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
. Simply substituting
u
for
w^{\prime}
may break the polytree shape of the sequent, e.g., if there is a
v
such that
wRv
and
vRu
are in
\mathcal{R}
, then replacing
u
for
w^{\prime}
in
wRw^{\prime}
would break the polytree shape of the sequent. So the relational atoms in the sequent also need to be modified. A transformation that we use in this case is represented by the rule
(br_{f})
, which shifts the relational atom
wRw^{\prime}
‘forward’ from
w
to
u
given that
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
. We also need another transformation to ‘merge’ the label
u
with the label
w^{\prime}
, deleting the relational atom in the process, represented as the
(mrg)
rule.

\pi_{7}

(iw)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash u:\varphi,u:\psi,\Delta

\pi_{1}^{\prime}

\mathcal{R},wRw^{\prime},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}:\varphi\vdash w^{\prime}:\psi,\Delta

(br_{f})

\mathcal{R},uRw^{\prime},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}:\varphi\vdash w^{\prime}:\psi,\Delta

(mrg)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u:\varphi\vdash u:\psi,\Delta

cut

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash u:\psi,\Delta

If we cut the above proof with
\pi_{8}
, we obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta.
Here we gloss over the termination arguments, but the details are available in the proof of Theorem
30
.

The above example illustrates one among several proof transformations needed in cut-elimination. These transformations make use of the auxiliary rules in Figure
3
. The bulk of the cut-elimination proof consists of showing these rules (height-preserving/hp-) admissible and the rules of
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
and
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
height-preserving invertible (i.e., hp-invertible). (NB. We take the notions of (hp-)admissibility and (hp-)invertibility to be defined as usual.) All (hp-)admissible rules preserve the polytree structure of sequents, and with the exception of
(gax)
,
(cut)
, and
(cd)
, all rules in Figure
3
are
hp-admissible
in
both
calculi. The
(gax)
and
(cut)
rules are strictly
admissible
in both calculi, while
(cd)
is hp-admissible in only
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
as the availability conditions are not imposed on rules, rendering domain atoms unnecessary (see Remark
14
). We now discuss some of the most interesting rules of Figure
3
.

Let us first explain the rules
(br_{f})
and
(br_{b})
. For some labels
w
,
u
, and
v
, assume
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
u\normalsize\not\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v
for
\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}^{\prime},{w}R{v}
. Then, we know that (1)
u
and
v
are on two different paths passing through
w
of the polytree generated from
\mathcal{R}
, and (2) there is no vertex between
v
and
w
since otherwise a cycle would be present in
\mathcal{R}
. In this scenario, the rule
(br_{f})
(for
br
anch
f
orward) allows one to move the polytree ‘rooted’ at
v
forward by connecting it to
u
instead of
w
as shown left in the below figure. The rule
(br_{b})
has a similar functionality; for some labels
w
,
u
, and
v
, assume
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
w\normalsize\not\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v
for
\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}^{\prime},{v}R{u}
. Then, the rule
(br_{b})
(for
br
anch
b
ackward) lets one move the polytree ‘rooted’ at
v
backward by connecting it to
w
instead of
u
as shown right in the below figure.

w
u
v
\Rightarrow
w
u
v

w
u
v
\Rightarrow
w
u
v

Figure 4:
The left and right diagrams demonstrate the functionality of
(br_{f})
and
(br_{b})
, respectively.

The
(mrg)
rule merges a label and its direct successor and corresponds to the rules
nodemergeD
and
nodemergeU
of Pinto and Uustalu
[
28
]
. The rule
(id)
reflects the redundancy of a variable labeled by two labels such that one is reachable by the other. Model-theoretically, this redundancy follows from the fact that if
x
is interpreted at
w
, and
u
is reachable from
w
(in a model), then
x
is interpreted at
u
as well, showing the domain atom
u:x
superfluous in the premise. Note that when the labels
w
and
u
are identical, then the rule represents a contraction on domain atoms; as
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}w
always holds, we have that identical domain atoms can always be contracted in sequents. The rules
(lwr)
and
(lft)
allow us to modify the labels of formulae in a sequent by looking at its underlying polytree. More precisely, reading
(lwr)
and
(lft)
bottom-up, if
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
we can both
lower
the label of
u\!:\!\Pi
on the right of the sequent to
w
, and
lift
the label of
w\!:\!\Sigma
on the left of the sequent to
u
.

Lemma 29
.

All non-initial rules in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
are hp-invertible.

Finally, we can prove the admissibility of the
(cut)
rule. As our proof proceeds via local transformations of proofs, the proof is constructive and yields a cut-elimination algorithm.

Theorem 30

(Cut-elimination)
.

The
(cut)
rule is admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

We proceed by a primary induction (PIH) on the complexity of the cut formula, and a secondary induction (SIH) on the sum of the heights of the proofs of the premises of
(cut)
. Assume that we have proofs of the following form, with
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
.

\pi_{1}

(r_{1})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi

\pi_{2}

(r_{2})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta

We argue that there is a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
by a case distinction on
(r_{1})
and
(r_{2})
, the last rules applied in the above proofs. We focus on some interesting cases; the remaining cases can be found in Appendix
B
.

(r_{1})=(ax):
Then
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v_{0}\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta_{0},v_{1}\!:\!p(\vec{t})
where
v_{0}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v_{1}
.
If
v_{1}\!:\!p(\vec{t})
is
w\!:\!\varphi
, then we have that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v_{0}\!:\!p(\vec{t}),u\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0},v_{0}\!:\!p(\vec{t})
. Given that
v_{0}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v_{1}
and
v_{1}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
, we can apply the hp-admissibility of
(lft)
on the latter to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v_{0}\!:\!p(\vec{t}),v_{0}\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
. Consequently, we obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v_{0}\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
, i.e. of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, using the hp-admissibility of
(ctr_{l})
. If
v_{1}\!:\!p(\vec{t})
is not
w\!:\!\varphi
, then we have that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v_{0}\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta_{0},v_{1}\!:\!p(\vec{t})
where
v_{0}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v_{1}
. The latter is provable by the admissibility of
(gax)
.

(r_{1})=(ds):
Then
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi
and we have a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!VT(\vec{t}),\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi
.
Consequently, we know that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
. We also have that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t}),u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta
.
We can repeatedly apply the hp-admissibility of
(wv)
on the proof of the latter to obtain a proof of

S:=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!VT(\vec{t}),\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t}),u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta
. Then, we proceed as follows:

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!VT(\vec{t}),\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi

S

SIH

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!VT(\vec{t}),\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

(ds)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

Note that the instance of SIH is justified because the sum of the heights of the proofs of the premises has decreased.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}}{\mathrm{L}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma_{0},v\!:\!\psi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.04306pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\chi\vdash\Delta

(r_{1})=(\exists{\mathrm{R}}):
Then there are two cases to consider: in the left premise
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi
of
(cut)
, either (1)
w\!:\!\varphi
is not the principal formula
v\!:\!\exists x\psi
, or (2)
w\!:\!\varphi
is the principal formula. In case (1), we have a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta_{1},v\!:\!\exists x\psi,v\!:\!\psi(t/x),w\!:\!\varphi
, which we call
S
, and
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta_{1},v\!:\!\exists x\psi
. We proceed as follows.

S

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta_{1},v\!:\!\exists x\psi

Lem.
29

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta_{1},v\!:\!\exists x\psi,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)

SIH

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta_{1},v\!:\!\exists x\psi,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)

(\exists{\mathrm{R}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta_{1},v\!:\!\exists x\psi

In case (2), we have proof a of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,v\!:\!\exists x\psi,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)
, and
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta
is of the form
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\exists x\psi\vdash\Delta
. In this case, we need to consider the shape of
(r_{2})
. If
u\!:\!\exists x\psi
is not principal in
(r_{2})
, then we apply the hp-invertibility of
(r_{2})
(
Lemma

29
) to the left premise of
(cut)
and use SIH to cut the result with the premise of
(r_{2})
, applying
(r_{2})
afterward to reach our goal. If
u\!:\!\exists x\psi
is principal in
(r_{2})
, then the premise of
(r_{2})
is of the shape
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!y,\Gamma,v\!:\!\psi(y/x)\vdash\Delta
where
y
is fresh. Then, we proceed as follows where
\pi
is the first proof given and
x_{0},\dots,x_{n}
are all the variables appearing in
t
.

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,v\!:\!\exists x\psi,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\exists x\psi\vdash\Delta

(iw)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u\!:\!\exists x\psi\vdash\Delta,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)

SIH

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)

\pi

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!y,\Gamma,v\!:\!\psi(y/x)\vdash\Delta

(t/y)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!x_{0},\dots,v\!:\!x_{n},\Gamma,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)\vdash\Delta

(id)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,v\!:\!\psi(t/x)\vdash\Delta

PIH

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta

Note that the step involving
(id)
is justified as
t
is available for
v
, meaning for each
x_{i}\in VT(t)
, there exists a domain atom
u_{i}:x_{i}
such that
u_{i}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}v
, showing
(id)
applicable.
∎

5
Concluding Remarks

Our analysis indicates that there may be two interesting and possibly distinct first-order extensions of bi-intuitionistic logic that may be worth exploring. The first is to consider a logic with decreasing domains, i.e., if
w\leq u
then
D(u)\subseteq D(w)
in the Kripke model. Semantically, this logic is easy to define, but its proof theory is not at all obvious. We are looking into the possibility of formalizing a notion of “non-existence predicate,” that is dual to the existence predicate, suggested by Restall
[
33
]
. This non-existence predicate may play a similar (but dual) role to the existence predicate in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID}).
The other extension is motivated from the proof theoretical perspective. As mentioned in Remark
19
, it seems that one can obtain a subsystem of
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
without the domain-shift rule
(ds)
that satisfies cut-elimination. As discussed in Section
3
, the
(ds)
rule is crucial to ensure the completeness of
\mathsf{BIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
in the presence of the exclusion operator, and so, a natural question to ask is what the semantics of such a logic would look like.

Appendix A
Soundness and Completeness Proofs

A.1
Soundness

Theorem
20

(Soundness)
.

Let
S
be a sequent. If
S
is provable in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
(
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
), then
S
is (
\mathrm{CD}
-)valid.

Proof.

We argue the claim by induction on the height of the given derivation and consider the
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
case as the
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
case is similar.

Base case.
It is straightforward to show that any instance of
(\bot{\mathrm{L}})
or
(\top{\mathrm{R}})
is valid; hence, we focus on
(ax)
and show that any instance thereof is valid. Let us consider the following instance of
(ax)
, where
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
due to the side condition imposed on
(ax)
, that is, there exist
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R})
such that
wRv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}Ru\in\mathcal{R}
.

(ax)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!p(\vec{t})

Let us suppose
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!p(\vec{t})
is invalid, i.e., a model
M=(W,\leq,U,D,I_{1},I_{2})
,
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment
\alpha
exist such that the following hold:
\iota(w)\leq\iota(v_{1}),\ldots,\iota(v_{n})\leq\iota(u)
,
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash p(\vec{t})
, and
M,\iota(u),\alpha\not\Vdash p(\vec{t})
. By the monotonicity condition
\mathrm{(M)}
(see Definition
3
), it must be that
M,\iota(u),\alpha\Vdash p(\vec{t})
, giving a contradiction. Thus, every instance of
(ax)
must be valid.

Inductive step.
We prove the inductive step by contraposition, showing that if the conclusion of the last inference in the given proof is invalid, then at least one premise of the final inference must be invalid. We make a case distinction based on the last rule applied in the given derivation.

(ds)
. Suppose
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
is invalid with
\vec{t}=t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
. Then, there exists a model
M
,
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment
\alpha
such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash p(\vec{t})
. Therefore,
(\overline{\alpha}(t_{1}),\ldots,\overline{\alpha}(t_{n}))\in I_{2}(w,p)
, and since
I_{2}(w,p)\subseteq D(w)^{n}
, we have that
\overline{\alpha}(t_{i})\in D(\iota(w))
for
1\leq i\leq n
. By the
\mathrm{(C_{1})}
and
\mathrm{(C_{2})}
conditions, we know that
M,\iota,\alpha\models w\!:\!VT(\vec{t})
. Therefore,
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},w\!:\!VT(\vec{t}),\Gamma,w\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
is invalid as well.

(\land{\mathrm{L}})
. If we assume that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\varphi\land\psi\vdash\Delta
is invalid, then there exists a model
M
,
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment
\alpha
such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash\varphi\land\psi
, implying that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash\varphi
and
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash\psi
, showing that the premise
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\varphi,w\!:\!\psi\vdash\Delta
is invalid as well.

(\land{\mathrm{R}})
. Let us suppose that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi\land\psi
is invalid. Then, there exists an model
M
,
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment
\alpha
such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\not\Vdash\varphi\land\psi
. Hence, either
M,\iota(w),\alpha\not\Vdash\varphi
or
M,\iota(w),\alpha\not\Vdash\psi
. In the first case, the left premise of
(\land{\mathrm{R}})
is invalid, and in the second case, the right premise of
(\land{\mathrm{R}})
is invalid.

(\lor{\mathrm{L}})
. Similar to the
(\land{\mathrm{R}})
case.

(\lor{\mathrm{R}})
. Similar to the
(\land{\mathrm{L}})
case.

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
. Assume
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\varphi\rightarrow\psi\vdash\Delta
is invalid and
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
, i.e. a sequence
wRv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}Ru
of relational atoms exist in
\mathcal{R}
. By our assumption, there exists a model
M
,
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment such that
\iota(w)\leq\iota(v_{1}),\ldots,\iota(v_{n})\leq\iota(u)
and
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Because
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
and
\leq
is transitive, we know that either
M,\iota(u),\alpha\not\Vdash\varphi
or
M,\iota(u),\alpha\Vdash\psi
. In the first case, the left premise of
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
is invalid, and in the second case, the right premise of
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
is invalid.

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
. Assume that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\varphi\rightarrow\psi
is invalid. Then, there exists a model
M
, an
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and an
M
-assignment
\alpha
such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\not\Vdash\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. Hence, there exists a world
u
such that
\iota(w)\leq u
,
M,u,\alpha\Vdash\varphi
, and
M,u,\alpha\not\Vdash\psi
. Let
\iota^{\prime}(v)=\iota(v)
for all labels
v\neq u
and
\iota^{\prime}(u)=u
otherwise. Then,
M
,
\iota^{\prime}
, and
\alpha
falsify the premise of
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
, showing it invalid.

({\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}}{\mathrm{L}})
. Similar to the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
case above.

({\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.04306pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}}{\mathrm{R}})
. Similar to the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
case above.

(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
. Suppose that
S=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\exists x\varphi\vdash\Delta
is invalid. Then, there exists a model
M
, an
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and an
M
-assignment
\alpha
such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash\exists x\varphi
. Therefore, there exists an
a\in D(\iota(w))
such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha[a/y]\Vdash\varphi(y/x)
with
y
not occurring in
S
. Then, as
y
is fresh,
M
,
\iota
, and
\alpha[a/y]
falsify the premise of
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
, showing it invalid.

(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
. Suppose that
S=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\exists x\varphi
is invalid. Then, there exists a model
M
, and
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\not\Vdash\exists x\varphi
. By the side condition on
(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
, we know that
\mathsf{A}(t,X_{w},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T})
, meaning there exist labels
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathrm{Lab}(S)
such that
u_{1}:x_{1},\ldots,u_{n}:x_{n}\in\mathcal{T}
,

, and
u_{1}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}w,\ldots,u_{n}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}w
. It follows that
\iota(u_{1})\leq\iota(w),\ldots,\iota(u_{n})\leq\iota(w)
and
\overline{\alpha}(x_{1})\in D(\iota(u_{1})),\ldots,\overline{\alpha}(x_{n})\in D(\iota(u_{n}))
. By the increasing domain condition
\mathrm{(ID)}
, we have that
\overline{\alpha}(x_{1})\in D(\iota(w)),\ldots,\overline{\alpha}(x_{n})\in D(\iota(w))
. Therefore, by the
\mathrm{(C_{1})}
and
\mathrm{(C_{2})}
conditions, we know that
\overline{\alpha}(t)\in D(\iota(w))
, showing that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\not\Vdash\varphi(t/x)
, and thus, the premise is invalid.

(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
. Suppose that
S=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\forall x\varphi\vdash\Delta
is invalid. Then, there exists a model
M
, and
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment
\alpha
such that
M,\iota(w),\alpha\Vdash\forall x\varphi
. By the side condition on
(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
, we know that
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
\mathsf{A}(t,X_{w},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T})
. By the latter fact, there exist labels
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathrm{Lab}(S)
such that

,

, and

. It follows that
\iota(v_{1})\leq\iota(w),\ldots,\iota(v_{n})\leq\iota(w)
and
\overline{\alpha}(x_{1})\in D(\iota(v_{1})),\ldots,\overline{\alpha}(x_{n})\in D(\iota(v_{n}))
. By the increasing domain condition
\mathrm{(ID)}
, we have that
\overline{\alpha}(x_{1})\in D(\iota(w)),\ldots,\overline{\alpha}(x_{n})\in D(\iota(w))
. Therefore, by the
\mathrm{(C_{1})}
and
\mathrm{(C_{2})}
conditions and our assumption, we know that
\overline{\alpha}(t)\in D(\iota(w))
, showing that

. By the fact that

, we know

and
\overline{\alpha}(t)\in D(\iota(u))
, showing that

by Proposition

. Thus, the premise is invalid.

(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
. Let us assume that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w\!:\!\forall x\varphi
is invalid. Then, there exists a model
M
, an

-interpretation

, and an

such that

. Thus, there exists a world

such that

,

, and

. We define

if

and

. Then,

,

, and

falsify the premise

, showing it invalid.
∎

A.2
Completeness

We prove the cut-free completeness of
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
by showing that if a sequent of the form
w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
is not provable, then a counter-model can be constructed witnessing the invalidity of the sequent. We focus on the proof for
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
as it is more involved than the similar proof for
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
. Our proof makes use of various new notions, which we now define. A
pseudo-derivation
is defined to be a (potentially infinite) tree whose nodes are sequents and where every parent node corresponds to the conclusion of a rule in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
with the children nodes corresponding to the premises. We remark that a proof in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
is a finite pseudo-derivation where all sequents at leaves are instances of
(ax)
,
(\bot{\mathrm{L}})
, or
(\top{\mathrm{R}})
. A
branch

\mathcal{B}
is defined to be a maximal path of sequents through a pseudo-derivation, starting from the conclusion. The following lemma is useful in our proof of completeness.

Lemma
21
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{ID},\mathcal{CD}\}
. For each
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, let
S_{i}=\mathcal{R}_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i},\Gamma_{i}\vdash\Delta_{i}
be a sequent.

1.

If
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
holds for the conclusion of a rule
(r)
in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
, then
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
holds for the premises of
(r)
;

2.

If
w:p(\vec{t})\in\Gamma_{0},\Delta_{0}
and
S_{0}
is the conclusion of a rule
(r)
in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
with
S_{1}
(and
S_{2}
) the premise(s) of
(r)
, then
w:p(\vec{t})\in\Gamma_{1},\Delta_{1}
(and
w:p(\vec{t})\in\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{2}
, resp.);

3.

If
w:x\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
S_{0}
is the conclusion of a rule
(r)
in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
with
S_{1}
(and
S_{2}
) the premise(s) of
(r)
, then
w:x\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
(and
w:x\in\mathcal{T}_{2}
, resp.).

Proof.

Each claim can be seen to hold by inspecting the rules of
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

The lemma tells us that propagation paths, the position of atomic formulae, and the position of terms are bottom-up preserved in rule applications.

Theorem
22
.

If
w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
is (
\mathrm{CD}
-)valid, then
w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
is provable in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
(
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{CD})
).

Proof.

We assume that
S=w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
is not derivable in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
and show that a model
M
can be defined which witnesses that
S
is invalid. To prove this, we first define a proof-search procedure
\mathtt{Prove}
that bottom-up applies rules from
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
to
w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
. Second, we show how an
M
can be extracted from failed proof-search. We now describe the proof-search procedure
\mathtt{Prove}
and let
\prec
be a well-founded, strict linear order over the set
\mathrm{Ter}
of terms.

\mathtt{Prove}
. Let us take
w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
as input and continue to the next step.

(ax)
,
(\bot{\mathrm{L}})
, and
(\top{\mathrm{R}})
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we halt the computation of
\mathtt{Prove}
on each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
where
S_{i}
is of the form
(ax)
,
(\bot{\mathrm{L}})
, or
(\top{\mathrm{R}})
. If
\mathtt{Prove}
is halted on each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
, then
\mathtt{Prove}
returns
\mathtt{True}
because a proof of the input has been constructed. However, if
\mathtt{Prove}
did not halt on each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, then let
\mathcal{B}_{j_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{j_{k}}
be the remaining branches for which
\mathtt{Prove}
did not halt. For each such branch, copy the top sequent above itself, and continue to the next step.

(ds)
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and extend the branch with bottom-up applications of
(ds)
rules. Let
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
be the current branch under consideration, and assume that
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
have already been considered. We assume that the top sequent in
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
is of the form

S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w_{1}:p_{1}(\vec{t}_{1}),\ldots,w_{\ell}:p_{\ell}(\vec{t}_{\ell})\vdash\Delta

where all atomic input formulae are displayed in
S_{k+1}
above. We successively consider each atomic input formula and bottom-up apply
(ds)
, yielding a branch extending
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
with a top sequent saturated under
(ds)
applications. After these operations have been performed for each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, we continue to the next step.

(\lor{\mathrm{L}})
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and extend the branch with bottom-up applications of
(\lor{\mathrm{L}})
rules. Let
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
be the current branch under consideration, and assume that
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
have already been considered. We assume that the top sequent in
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
is of the form

S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w_{1}:\varphi_{1}\lor\psi_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:\varphi_{m}\lor\psi_{m}\vdash\Delta

where all disjunctive formulae
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\lor\psi_{i}
are displayed in
S_{k+1}
above. We consider each labeled formula
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\lor\psi_{i}
in turn, and bottom-up apply the
(\lor{\mathrm{L}})
rule, which gives
2^{m}
new branches extending
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
such that each branch has a top sequent of the form
S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w_{1}:\chi_{1}\ldots,w_{m}:\chi_{m}\vdash\Delta
with
\chi_{i}\in\{\varphi_{i},\psi_{i}\}
and
1\leq i\leq m
. After these operations have been performed for each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, we continue to the next step.

(\lor{\mathrm{R}})
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and extend the branch with bottom-up applications of
(\lor{\mathrm{R}})
rules. Let
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
be the current branch under consideration, and assume that
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
have already been considered. We assume that the top sequent in
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
is of the form

S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w_{1}:\varphi_{1}\lor\psi_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:\varphi_{m}\lor\psi_{m},\Delta

where all disjunctive formulae
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\lor\psi_{i}
are displayed in
S_{k+1}
above. We consider each labeled formula
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\lor\psi_{i}
in turn, and bottom-up apply the
(\lor{\mathrm{R}})
rule. These
(\lor{\mathrm{R}})
rule applications extend
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
such that
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w_{1}:\varphi_{1},w_{1}:\psi_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:\varphi_{m},w_{m}:\psi_{m},\Delta
is now the top sequent of the branch. After these operations have been performed for each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, we continue to the next step.

(\land{\mathrm{L}})
. Similar to the
(\lor{\mathrm{R}})
case above.

(\land{\mathrm{R}})
. Similar to the
(\lor{\mathrm{L}})
case above.

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and extend the branch with bottom-up applications of
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
rules. Let
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
be the current branch under consideration, and assume that
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
have already been considered. We assume that the top sequent in
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
is of the form

S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w_{1}:\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\psi_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:\varphi_{m}\rightarrow\psi_{m}\vdash\Delta

where all implicational formulae
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\psi_{i}
are displayed in
S_{k+1}
above. We consider each formula
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\psi_{i}
in turn, and bottom-up apply the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
rule. Let
w_{\ell+1}:\varphi_{\ell+1}\rightarrow\psi_{\ell+1}
be the current formula under consideration, and assume that
w_{1}:\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\psi_{1},\ldots,w_{\ell}:\varphi_{\ell}\rightarrow\psi_{\ell}
have already been considered. For every label
u
such that
w_{\ell+1}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
, bottom-up apply the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
rule. After these operations have been performed for each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, we continue to the next step.

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and extend the branch with bottom-up applications of
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
rules. Let
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
be the current branch under consideration, and assume that
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
have already been considered. We assume that the top sequent in
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
is of the form

S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w_{1}:\varphi_{1}\rightarrow\psi_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:\varphi_{m}\rightarrow\psi_{m},\Delta

where all implicational formulae
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\psi_{i}
are displayed in
S_{k+1}
above. We consider each formula
w_{i}:\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\psi_{i}
in turn, and bottom-up apply the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
rule. These
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
rule applications extend
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
such that

\mathcal{R},w_{1}Ru_{1},\ldots,w_{m}Ru_{m},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u_{1}:\varphi_{1},\ldots,u_{m}:\varphi_{m}\vdash u_{1}:\psi_{1},\ldots,u_{m}:\psi_{m},\Delta

is now the top sequent of the branch with
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
fresh. After these operations have been performed for each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, we continue to the next step.

({\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.04306pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}}{\mathrm{L}})
. Similar to the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
case above.

({\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.04306pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}}{\mathrm{R}})
. Similar to the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
case above.

(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and extend the branch with bottom-up applications of
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
rules. Let
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
be the current branch under consideration, and assume that
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
have already been considered. We assume that the top sequent in
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
is of the form

S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w_{1}:\exists x_{1}\varphi_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:\exists x_{m}\varphi_{m}\vdash\Delta

where all existential input formulae
w_{i}:\exists x_{i}\varphi_{i}
are displayed in
S_{k+1}
above. We consider each formula
w_{i}:\exists x_{i}\varphi_{i}
in turn, and bottom-up apply the
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
rule. These
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
rule applications extend
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
such that

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},w_{1}:y_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:y_{m},\Gamma,w_{1}:\varphi_{1}(y_{1}/x_{1}),\ldots,w_{n}:\varphi_{m}(y_{m}/x_{m})\vdash\Delta

is now the top sequent of the branch with
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
fresh variables. After these operations have been performed for each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, we continue to the next step.

(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
. Suppose
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
are all branches occurring in the current pseudo-derivation and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each respective branch. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we consider
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and extend the branch with bottom-up applications of
(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
rules. Let
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
be the current branch under consideration, and assume that
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{k}
have already been considered. We assume that the top sequent in
\mathcal{B}_{k+1}
is of the form

S_{k+1}=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash w_{1}:\exists x_{1}\varphi_{1},\ldots,w_{m}:\exists x_{m}\varphi_{m},\Delta

where all existential formulae
w_{i}:\exists x_{i}\varphi_{i}
are displayed in
S_{k+1}
above. We consider each labeled formula
w_{m}:\exists x_{m}\varphi_{i}
in turn, and bottom-up apply the
(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
rule. Let
w_{\ell+1}:\exists x_{\ell+1}\varphi_{\ell+1}
be the current formula under consideration, and assume that
w_{1}:\exists x_{1}\varphi_{1},\ldots,w_{\ell}:\exists x_{\ell}\varphi_{\ell}
have already been considered. Recall that
\prec
is a well-founded, strict linear order over the set
\mathrm{Ter}
of terms. Choose the
\prec
-minimal term
t\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{w_{\ell+1}})
that has yet to be picked to instantiate
w_{\ell+1}:\exists x_{\ell+1}\varphi_{\ell+1}
and bottom-up apply the
(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
rule, thus adding
w_{\ell+1}:\varphi_{\ell+1}(t/x_{\ell+1})
. After these operations have been performed for each branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq n
, we continue to the next step.

(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
. Similar to the
(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
case above.

(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
. Similar to the
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
and
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
cases above. We now loop back to the ‘
(ax)
,
(\bot{\mathrm{L}})
, and
(\top{\mathrm{R}})
’ step.

This concludes the description of
\mathtt{Prove}
.

We now argue that if
\mathtt{Prove}
does not return
\mathtt{True}
, then a model
M
,
M
-interpretation
\iota
, and
M
-assignment
\alpha
can be defined such that
M,\iota,\alpha\not\models S
. If
\mathtt{Prove}
halts, i.e.
\mathtt{Prove}
returns
\mathtt{True}
, then a proof of
S
may be obtained by ‘contracting’ all redundant inferences from the ‘
(ax)
,
(\bot{\mathrm{L}})
, and
(\top{\mathrm{R}})
’ step of
\mathtt{Prove}
, which contradicts our assumption. Therefore,
\mathtt{Prove}
does not halt, that is,
\mathtt{Prove}
generates an infinite tree with finite branching. By König’s lemma, an infinite branch must exist in this infinite tree, which we denote by
\mathcal{B}
. We define a model
M=(W,\leq,U,D,I_{1},I_{2})
by means of this branch.

First, let us define the following multisets, all of which are obtained by taking the union of each multiset of relational atoms, domain atoms, antecedent labeled formulae, and consequent labeled formulae (resp.) occurring within a sequent in
\mathcal{B}
:

\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bigcup_{(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{B}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{R}\qquad\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bigcup_{(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{B}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathcal{T}\qquad\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bigcup_{(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{B}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\Gamma\qquad\Delta^{\mathcal{B}}=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bigcup_{(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{B}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\Delta

•

u\in W

iff

u\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}},\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}},\Delta^{\mathcal{B}})
;

•

\leq\ =\ \{(u,v)\ |\ uRv\in\mathcal{R}\}^{*}
where
*
denotes the reflexive-transitive closure;

•

t\in U

iff
there exists a label
u\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}},\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}},\Delta^{\mathcal{B}})
such that
t\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
;

•

t\in D(u)

iff

t\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
;

•

(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in I_{2}(u,p)

iff

v,u\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}\!,\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}}\!,\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}\!,\Delta^{\mathcal{B}})
,
v\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}u
, and
v:p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}\!.

We now verify that
M
is indeed a model. Observe that
W\neq\emptyset
since
w\in W
by definition and the relation
\leq
is reflexive and transitive by definition. Furthermore, by definition,
D(u)\subseteq U
for each
u\in W
and
U=\bigcup_{u\in W}D(u)
. Also, since our language contains at least one constant symbol
a
(see Remark
16
), we know that
a\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
for each
u\in W
, i.e. for each
u\in W
,
D(u)\neq\emptyset
. Let us now argue that
M
satisfies the increasing domain condition
\mathrm{(ID)}
, and assume
u,v\in W
,
t\in D(u)
, and
u\leq v
. Since
t\in D(u)
,
t\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
, and since
u\leq v
, we know that
u\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}v
. It follows that
\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})\subseteq\mathrm{Ter}(X_{v})
by Definition
13
, showing that
t\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{v})
, and thus,
t\in D(v)
. It is simple to confirm that
I_{1}
satisfies the
\mathrm{(C_{1})}
and
\mathrm{(C_{2})}
conditions as
t\in D(u)

iff

t\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
, and
\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
contains every constant and is closed under the formation of terms by definition. For each
n
-ary predicate
p
and world
u\in W
,
I_{2}(p,u)\subseteq D(u)^{n}
. To show this, suppose that
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in I_{2}(p,u)
. Then, there exists a label
v\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}},\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}},\Delta^{\mathcal{B}})
such that
v\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}u
, and
v:p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=v:p(\vec{t})\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
. By the
(ds)
step of
\mathtt{Prove}
, we know that
w:VT(\vec{t})\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}}
. It follows that
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
, implying that
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in D(u)^{n}
. Finally, we argue that
M
satisfies the monotonicity condition
\mathrm{(M)}
, and therefore, we assume
u,v\in W
,
u\leq v
, and
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in I_{2}(p,u)
. Since
u\leq v
, we know that there exist
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}},\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}},\Delta^{\mathcal{B}})
such that
uRw_{1},\ldots,w_{n}Rv
, implying that
u\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}v
. Since
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in I_{2}(p,u)
, there exists a
v^{\prime}
such that
v^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}u
and
v^{\prime}:p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
. Hence,
v^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}v
because
v^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}u
and
u\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}v
, which shows that
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in I_{2}(p,v)
.

We have now confirmed that
M
is indeed a model. Let us define
\alpha
to be the
M
-assignment mapping every variable in
U
to itself and every variable in
\mathrm{Var}\setminus U
arbitrarily. To finish the proof of completeness, we now argue the following by mutual induction on the complexity of
\psi
: (1) if
u:\psi\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
, then
M,u,\alpha\Vdash\psi
, and (2) if
u:\psi\in\Delta^{\mathcal{B}}
, then
M,u,\alpha\not\Vdash\psi
. We argue the cases where
\psi
is of the form
p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
or
\forall x\chi
, and omit the remaining cases as they are straightforward or similar.

•

u:p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
. In this case,
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in I_{2}(p,u)
by the definition of
I_{2}
, implying that
M,u,\alpha\Vdash p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
.

•

u:p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\Delta^{\mathcal{B}}
. Observe that if a label
v\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}},\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}},\Delta^{\mathcal{B}})
exists such that
v\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}u
and
v:p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
, then due to the ‘
(ax)
,
(\bot{\mathrm{L}})
, and
(\top{\mathrm{R}})
’ step of
\mathtt{Prove}
,
\mathcal{B}
would be finite. However, as this is not the case, it must be that no label
v\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{B}},\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}},\Delta^{\mathcal{B}})
exists such that
v\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}u
and
v:p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
, showing that
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\not\in I_{2}(u,p)
, i.e.
M,u,\alpha\not\Vdash p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
.

•

u:\forall x\chi\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
. Suppose
v\in W
,
t\in D(v)
, and
u\leq v
. By the assumption that
t\in D(v)
, we know that
\mathsf{A}(t,X_{u},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T})
holds for some sequent
S=\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
in
\mathcal{B}
. Let us suppose w.l.o.g. that
S
is the first such sequent in
\mathcal{B}
for which this holds. By Lemma
21
, it follows that
\mathsf{A}(t,X_{w},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
holds for every sequent
S^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}
above
S
in
\mathcal{B}
. By the assumption that
u\leq v
, we know that
u\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{B}}}v
. Hence, at some point the
(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
step of
\mathtt{Prove}
will be applicable at or above
S
in
\mathcal{B}
, meaning
v:\chi(t/x)\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{B}}
. By IH, we have that
M,v,\alpha\Vdash\chi(t/x)
, from which it follows that
M,w,\alpha\Vdash\forall x\chi
by our assumptions.

•

u:\forall x\chi\in\Delta^{\mathcal{B}}
. Due to the
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
step of
\mathtt{Prove}
, a sequent of the form
\mathcal{R},{u}R{v},\mathcal{T},v\!:\!y,\Gamma\vdash\Delta,v\!:\!\chi(y/x)
with
v
and
y
fresh must occur in
\mathcal{B}
. By the definition of
\leq
and
D(v)
, as well as Lemma
21
, it follows that
u\leq v
and
y\in D(v)
. By IH and the definition of
\alpha
,
M,v,\alpha\not\Vdash\chi(y/x)
, and so,
M,u,\alpha\not\Vdash\forall x\chi
.

Let
\iota
to be the
M
-interpretation such that
\iota(u)=u
for
u\in W
and
\iota(v)\in W
for
v\not\in W
. By the proof above,
M,\iota,\alpha\not\models w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
, showing that if a sequent of the form
w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
is not derivable in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
, then it is invalid, that is, every valid sequent of the form
w:\vec{x}\vdash w:\varphi(\vec{x})
is provable in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{ID})
.
∎

Appendix B
Admissibility and Invertibility Proofs

Lemma 31
.

The
(gax)
rule is derivable (and therefore, admissible) in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

By induction on the complexity of the principal formula
\varphi
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

The rules
(br_{f})
and
(br_{b})
are hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

By changing
{w}R{v}
to
{u}R{v}
, we are effectively moving the polytree rooted at
v
from
w
to
u
as indicated in the diagram below.
w
u
v
\Huge\Rightarrow
\Huge\Rightarrow
w
u
v

The crucial observation is that given that
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
, this operation only extends the reachability relation: we have that for all
w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime\prime}
, if
w^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}w^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{R},{w}R{v}
then
w^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}w^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{R},{u}R{v}
. Consequently, we can see that
(br_{f})
only expands reachability, and thus does not violate the reachability or availability conditions of any of the rules. Freshness is also not impacted. So, in all cases we can simply apply the induction hypothesis on the premises, and then the rule.

A similar argument can be provided for
(br_{b})
, where we move from the left diagram to the right one below.
w
u
v
\Huge\Rightarrow
w
u
v

∎

Lemma 33
.

The
(mrg)
rule is hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the height of the given proof.
∎

Lemma 34
.

The
(id)
rule is hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

This lemma is straightforwardly proved by induction on the height of proofs. To realize that we can simply apply the induction hypothesis on the premises of the rules, and then reapply the rule, it suffices to note that the deletion of
u\!:\!x
does not impact reachability, freshness or even availability given that
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
. So, any rule is reapplicable once we use the induction hypothesis.
∎

Lemma 35
.

The
(t/x)
rule is hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

We reason by induction on the height of proofs, and consider the last rule applied.

Obviously, the propositional rules are not impacted by the substitution. So, for these rules we simply need to apply the induction hypothesis on the premises of the rule, and then reapply the rule.

The rule
(ds)
is treated straightforwardly: we apply the induction hypothesis on the proof of

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},w\!:\!VT(\vec{t^{\prime}}),\Gamma,w\!:\!p(\vec{t^{\prime}})\vdash\Delta
to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\Gamma(t/x),w\!:\!p(\vec{t^{\prime}})(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
where
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T},w\!:\!VT(\vec{t^{\prime}}))(t/x)
. Note that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is such that any domain atom
u\!:\!x
is replaced by
u\!:\!x_{0},\dots,u\!:\!x_{m}
where
x_{0},\dots,x_{m}
are the variables appearing in
t
. Consequently, all the variables appearing in the terms
\vec{t^{\prime}}(t/x)
are labeled by
w
in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Consequently, we can apply the rule
(ds)
to obtain a proof of our goal, i.e.
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(t/x),\Gamma(t/x),w:p(\vec{t^{\prime}})(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
.

The cases of rules for quantifiers deserve more attention. We show the cases of
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
and
(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
, as the other rules can be treated in a similar way.

(\exists{\mathrm{L}}):
we need to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R}(t/x),\mathcal{T}(t/x),\Gamma(t/x),w\!:\!(\exists z\varphi)(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
, where we can safely assume that
z
is different from
x
and any variable appearing in
t
. First, we apply the induction hypothesis on the proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},w\!:\!y,\Gamma,w\!:\!\varphi(y/z)\vdash\Delta
, the premise of the rule, to obtain a proof of no greater height of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(y^{\prime}/y),w\!:\!y(y^{\prime}/y),\Gamma(y^{\prime}/y),w\!:\!\varphi(y/z)(y^{\prime}/y)\vdash\Delta(y^{\prime}/y)
with
y^{\prime}
fresh and not appearing in
t
or
x
. Given that
y
was fresh, we have a proof of no greater height the sequent
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},w\!:\!y^{\prime},\Gamma,w\!:\!\varphi(y^{\prime}/z)\vdash\Delta
. We can apply the induction hypothesis again here to obtain a proof of no greater height of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(t/x),w\!:\!y^{\prime}(t/x),\Gamma(t/x),w\!:\!\varphi(y^{\prime}/z)(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
. Because of our choice of
y^{\prime}
, we have that the latter sequent is equal to
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(t/x),w\!:\!y^{\prime},\Gamma(t/x),w\!:\!\varphi(t/x)(y^{\prime}/z)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
. Thus, we can reapply the rule
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
on the latter to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R}(t/x),\mathcal{T}(t/x),\Gamma(t/x),w\!:\!(\exists z\varphi)(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
.

(\forall{\mathrm{L}}):
we need a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(t/x),\Gamma(t/x),w\!:\!(\forall z\varphi)(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
of no greater height, where we can safely assume that
z
is different from
x
and any variable appearing in
t
. We apply the induction hypothesis on the proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\forall z\varphi,u\!:\!\varphi(t^{\prime}/z)\vdash\Delta
, the premise of the rule, to obtain a proof of no greater height of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(t/x),\Gamma,w\!:\!(\forall z\varphi)(t/x),u\!:\!\varphi(t^{\prime}/z)(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
. Note that the latter is equal to
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(t/x),\Gamma,w\!:\!(\forall z\varphi)(t/x),u\!:\!\varphi(t^{\prime}(t/x)/z)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
. Clearly, in this sequent we still have that
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
. In addition to that, we have that
t^{\prime}(t/x)
is available for
u
. We can argue this point by a case distinction on the presence or not of
x
in
t^{\prime}
. If
x
does not appear in
t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}(t/x)=t^{\prime}
, which is available for
u
as initially given. If
x
does appear in
t^{\prime}
, then we have that there must be a
v
such that
v\!:\!x\in\mathcal{T}
and
v\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
else
t^{\prime}
would not be available in
u
. Consequently, we have that
v\!:\!x
is replaced by
\{v\!:\!x^{\prime}\mid x^{\prime}\text{ appears in }t\}
in
\mathcal{T}(t/x)
. This makes
t^{\prime}(t/x)
available for
u
. So, as
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
t^{\prime}(t/x)
is available to
u
we can apply
(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T}(t/x),\Gamma(t/x),w\!:\!(\forall z\varphi)(t/x)\vdash\Delta(t/x)
of no greater height than the proof initially considered.
∎

Lemma 36
.

The
(iw)
rule is hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

With the exception of the
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
or
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
rules, the
(iw)
rule permutes above all other rules of
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
. In the
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
and
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
cases, we can apply Lemma
35
to the premise of each rule to ensure that the freshness condition holds, then apply
(iw)
, and last, apply the
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
or
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
rule, respectively. Thus, the
(iw)
rule can be permuted upward in any
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
proof (in a height-preserving manner) and eliminated at initial sequents, showing the rule hp-admissible.
∎

Lemma 37
.

The
(wv)
rule is hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

The addition of a further labeled variable can only affect the freshness condition of an
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
or
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
rule. In such cases, we apply Lemma
35
, then
(wv)
, and last, the
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
or
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
rule, respectively, to permute
(wv)
above the
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
or
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
instance. In all other cases the
(wv)
rule freely permutes above the rule. Thus, the
(wv)
rule can be permuted upward in any
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
proof (in a height-preserving manner) and eliminated at initial sequents, showing the rule hp-admissible.
∎

Lemma 38
.

The rules
(\bot{\mathrm{R}})
and
(\top{\mathrm{L}})
are hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

We focus on
(\bot{\mathrm{R}})
as
(\top{\mathrm{L}})
can be treated dually. By inspecting the rules, one can see that the deletion of
w\!:\!\bot
in the consequent of a sequent impacts the application of no rule. In particular, this deletion does not alter the reachability, availability, or freshness conditions. As a consequence, a straightforward induction on the structure of the proof is sufficient to prove this statement: in the inductive cases, apply the induction hypothesis on the last rule applied, and then the rule.
∎

Lemma 39
.

The
(lwr)
rule is hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

We reason by induction on the height of proofs, and consider the last rule applied.

Obviously, the “local” propositional rules (which do not involve a condition on reachability) and
(ds)
are not impacted by the modification of the label, whether the principal formula is labeled by one of the labels under focus or not. For
({\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.04306pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}}{\mathrm{L}})
we easily reach our goal by applying the induction hypothesis on the premise and then the rule as the principal formula cannot be the one under focus.
However, the other non-local rules require more care.

(ax):
then we have a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta,u^{\prime}\!:\!p(\vec{t})
where
w^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u^{\prime}
. We proceed by case distinction on the equality between
w
and
u^{\prime}
. If
w=u^{\prime}
, then we need to prove
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}\!:\!p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!p(\vec{t})
. Now, note that we have
w^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
by assumption as
w=u^{\prime}
. Consequently, we have that
w^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
by transitivity. An application of the rule
(ax)
leads us to our goal. If
w\neq u^{\prime}
, then we can simply reapply the rule as the two labeled formulae are not modified.

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}}):
then the last rule has the following form.

\mathcal{R},{w^{\prime}}R{u^{\prime}},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta,u^{\prime}\!:\!\psi

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi\rightarrow\psi

If the principal formula is the labeled formula we intend to modify, then we have that
w^{\prime}=w
.
As we have
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and not
u\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u^{\prime}
because of the freshness of
u^{\prime}
, we can use Lemma
32
to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},{u}R{u^{\prime}},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi\vdash\Delta,u^{\prime}\!:\!\psi
. Then, it suffices to apply the rule
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{R}})
to reach a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!\varphi\rightarrow\psi
of the adequate height. If the principal formula is not the one we intend to modify, then we can use the induction hypothesis in the premise and reapply the rule.

({\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

then the last rule has the following form.

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.38751pt\hbox to8.06pt{\vbox to3.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.63937pt\lower-1.87936pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.50375pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.38751pt\hbox to8.06pt{\vbox to3.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.63937pt\lower-1.87936pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.50375pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.03876pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.24 0 L 9.87 2.57 L 10.24 2.24 L 8.31 0 L 10.24 -2.24 L 9.87 -2.57 L 7.24 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.38751pt}\psi

If the principal formula is not the one we intend to modify, then we can use the induction hypothesis in the premise and reapply the rule.
If the principal formula is the labeled formula we intend to modify, then we have that
w=u^{\prime}
.
As we have
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
w^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}w
by the rule application, we get
w^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
by transitivity. This allows us to apply the induction hypothesis in both premises to obtain proofs of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\psi,w^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi
and
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}\!:\!\psi\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

. Then, it suffices to reapply the rule to obtain a proof of adequate height of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.04306pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\psi
.

Let us now turn to the first order rules. For all the rules for quantifiers where the modified labeled formula cannot be principal, i.e. for
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
and
(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
, it suffices to apply the induction hypothesis in the premise, and then reapply the rule. So, we are left with the rules
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
and
(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
.

(\forall{\mathrm{R}}):
then the last rule has the following form.

\mathcal{R},{w^{\prime}}R{u^{\prime}},\mathcal{T},u^{\prime}\!:\!y,\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi(y/x)

(\forall{\mathrm{R}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w^{\prime}\!:\!\forall x\varphi

If the principal formula is not the one we intend to modify, then we can use the induction hypothesis in the premise and reapply the rule.
If the principal formula is the labeled formula we intend to modify, then we have that
w^{\prime}=w
.
As we have
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and not
u\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u^{\prime}
because of the freshness of
u^{\prime}
, we can use Lemma
32
to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},{u}R{u^{\prime}},\mathcal{T},u^{\prime}\!:\!y,\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi(y/x)
. Then, it suffices to apply the rule
(\forall{\mathrm{R}})
to reach a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!\forall x\varphi
of the adequate height.

(\exists{\mathrm{R}}):
then the last rule has the following form.

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w^{\prime}\!:\!\exists x\varphi,w^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi(t/x)

(\exists{\mathrm{R}})

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,w^{\prime}\!:\!\exists x\varphi

If the principal formula is not the one we intend to modify, then we can use the induction hypothesis in the premise and reapply the rule.
If the principal formula is the labeled formula we intend to modify, then we have that
w^{\prime}=w
.
Then, we can apply the induction hypothesis on the premise twice to obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma\vdash\Delta,u\!:\!\exists x\varphi,u\!:\!\varphi(t/x)
. Note that in this case, we have that
t\in\mathrm{Ter}(X_{u})
as
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
. So, we can apply the rule
(\exists{\mathrm{R}})
to reach our goal.

∎

Lemma 40
.

The
(lft)
rule is hp-admissible in
\mathsf{LBIQ}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is very similar in spirit to the proof of Lemma
39
. Dually, the problematic rules here are
(ds)
,
({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}})
,
({\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.55972pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.04306pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

,
(\exists{\mathrm{L}})
and
(\forall{\mathrm{L}})
.

(ds):
then the last rule has the following form where
u:p(\vec{t})
is the principal formula.

\pi_{1}

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u:p(\vec{t}),u:VT(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

(ds)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,u:p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

Applying the induction hypothesis to
\pi_{1}
, we get a proof
\pi_{2}
of

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:p(\vec{t}),u:VT(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
with the same height, from which we can obtain a proof of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta
as follows:

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:p(\vec{t}),u:VT(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

(wv)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:p(\vec{t}),w:VT(\vec{t}),u:VT(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

(id)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:p(\vec{t}),w:VT(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

(ds)

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w:p(\vec{t})\vdash\Delta

The instance of
(id)
above is applicable because
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
. The hp-admissibility of
(lft)
in this case then follows from Lemma
34
and Lemma
37

({\rightarrow}{\mathrm{L}}):
then the last rule has the following form.

\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w^{\prime}\!:\!\varphi\rightarrow\psi\vdash\Delta

u=w^{\prime}
.

w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u
and
u\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u^{\prime}
by the rule application, we get
w\twoheadrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}u^{\prime}
by transitivity. This allows us to apply the induction hypothesis in both premises to obtain proofs of

and
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\varphi\rightarrow\psi,u^{\prime}\!:\!\psi\vdash\Delta
. Then, it suffices to reapply the rule to obtain a proof of adequate height of
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{T},\Gamma,w\!:\!\varphi\rightarrow\psi\vdash\Delta
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
